- **Decision:** bounded negative (R-037). The contractual-barrier-hedging family is recorded in the
  known-baseline table (row 67); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no score
  changed; no validation specification was added; **and no new gap is opened**, because every reason above
  is independent of data availability.

---

### R-038 — FUNDSTATE (IDEA-002) — rejected: the funding-state proxy cannot be validated, and the candidate's state is not attachable to its own cross-section (Cycle 58)

- **What was closed.** **G-01**, IDEA-002's feasibility precondition and the reason the candidate has been
  parked as **P-004** since Cycle 5. The question was whether a public, defensible funding-state proxy can
  be constructed that demonstrably predicts disclosed dealer funding value adjustments over a multi-year
  sample. P-004's own entry states the consequence in advance: "If not, this candidate should be rejected
  rather than re-specified." IDEA-002's own prediction 4 states the same: the proxy "must be validated
  against disclosed dealer funding costs; if the proxy carries no information about them, the candidate is
  untestable and **must be dropped** rather than re-specified."
- **Why the answer is no — three independent reasons.**
  1. **The proposed validation is tautological for the candidate's own claim.** A dealer's funding value
     adjustment is *computed from its own funding curve* (S-010, S-155). A funding-cost proxy that
     "predicts" a disclosed FVA is therefore an accounting identity, not evidence that the proxy measures
     what IDEA-002 actually conditions on — **dealer-specific balance-sheet capacity**. S-297 shows the
     field is not even settled on the sign of the construct (whether FVA is a cost or a benefit of
     intermediation is an open argument), so the target is not unambiguously signed in the first place.
  2. **The target is thin, non-comparable and not multi-year.** Dealer FVAs are disclosed as annual,
     point-in-time amounts, by a small number of disclosing dealers, at materially different levels of
     materiality and under differing definitions; no continuous public series exists (S-155's caveat, which
     this cycle re-confirmed rather than contradicted); and the published tabulation of dealer FVA
     disclosures is a short window rather than a multi-year series. A validation over "a multi-year sample"
     is not constructible from published dealer disclosure at all.
  3. **The state the candidate needs is dealer-specific; every public ingredient is market-wide.** The
     public ingredients are market funding-cost series; the closest *dealer-specific* public measures are
     the Federal Reserve's quarterly Senior Credit Officer Opinion Survey (qualitative net percentages) and
     the NY Fed's daily SOFR statistics (S-155). A market-wide composite cannot measure one dealer's
     balance-sheet capacity; and with a market-wide state the conditioning changes only the **size** of an
     effect — failing G-12's sign-and-existence limb — while the relation it would condition is published
     (S-009) and its nearest published empirical form is a **seller-side** funding-state → flow impact →
     reversal result (S-292, S-293): the published way to condition flow impact on a funding state uses
     the *seller's* redemption pressure, not the intermediary's funding cost. S-297 adds the
     practitioner-level view that FVA belongs in fair value, which is IDEA-002's own premise, already
     documented.
- **Why this is a rejection and not a re-specification.** A market-wide funding composite would satisfy
  G-13's publication test but would (i) make the state common to every security, so the trade becomes a
  market-beta position rather than a stock-selection edge; (ii) condition only the *size* of an effect
  whose existence and sign are published (S-009, S-292, S-293) — the register's "baseline re-description
  with an extra regressor" pattern; and (iii) be collinear with the candidate's *own named rival
  conditioner*, contemporaneous volatility and depth, which the dossier lists as the kill condition for
  prediction 1 — a public funding composite is inseparable from volatility in stress, so the conditioning
  is not identified. Re-specifying around a weaker proxy is exactly what IDEA-002's dossier, its parking
  entry and the Cycle-57 handoff all forbid. **The candidate is therefore rejected on its own stated
  terms.**
- **What the register keeps.** The reusable components are filed as baselines: the dealer funding-value
  adjustment as a debt-overhang cost priced into dealer quotations (S-010, S-155, S-297); the
  state-dependence of market liquidity on funding constraints (S-009); the published *seller-side*
  funding-state → flow price impact and reversal relation (S-292, S-293); and the fund-liquidity and
  cross-sectional-arbitrage constructs (S-291).
- **Dated re-open condition.** Re-open only with a **new mechanism** — not a better wording and not a new
  proxy. Two specific triggers would qualify: (i) a **public, continuous, multi-year, dealer-level funding
  state** becomes available (for example a supervisory or regulator-published dealer funding-cost series),
  which would make G-01's validation constructible for the first time; or (ii) a published study
  demonstrates that the *intermediary's* funding state — as opposed to the seller's — conditions the
  flow-impact slope, which would supply the evidence G-01 was designed to look for.
- **Decision:** **rejected (R-038).** Score withdrawn (63.0). No `V-` specification was ever issued for
  IDEA-002, so none is withdrawn. **P-004 is closed.** The register now holds **five** active dossiers
  (IDEA-004, IDEA-009, IDEA-010, IDEA-011, IDEA-014), **five** parked (IDEA-003, IDEA-005, IDEA-008,
  IDEA-013, IDEA-015) and **five** rejected (IDEA-001, IDEA-002, IDEA-006, IDEA-007, IDEA-012).


### R-039 — Published price of a constraint (repo specialness / securities lending fee curve / CDS financing spread / standing repo award rate) — bounded negative (Cycle 59)

**Family screened (state-first):** continuously published, decision-time rates that price funding/balance-sheet/capacity constraints (repo specialness, securities-lending fee curves, CDS financing spreads, standing-repo award rates). Template (A) from Cycle-59 priorities.

**Reasons (independent):**
1. **Published baseline/indicator, not a clean unclaimed edge:** Public funding/capacity rates exist (S-041 SOFR/BGCR/TGCR; S-042 repo operations/primary dealer statistics) but they are largely indicator rates/policy-adjacent or aggregated; the literature treats them as states that condition effects rather than as unclaimed edges with a clean, sign-determining condition not already published.
2. **State availability/continuity:** Clean, continuously published decision-time series of securities-lending fee curves at the granularity needed for many candidate designs are largely proprietary/fragmented; repo specialness is episodic/specialized. Public coverage does not obviously meet the bar for a broad unclaimed family.
3. **G-12 fails (sign/existence):** The family conditions magnitude in published work more often than it changes sign/existence of an effect; no unclaimed sign-determining mechanism survived the strict bar.
4. **Anticipation/competition:** Where rates are public and widely observed, effects are anticipated or competed away.
5. **No feasible clean test with published state:** Without a clean published decision-time state that meets the Cycle-52 third test, no candidate could pass the strict bar.

**Dated re-screen condition:** Re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time series of a funding/balance-sheet/capacity constraint becomes available at the granularity the family needs (with documented cadence/coverage) and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-039).** The published-price-of-a-constraint family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 68**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no `S-` identifier was consumed; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because every reason above is independent of data availability — the screen failed on the *sign-and-existence* limb (reason 3) and on the *already-claimed* limb (reason 1), not on a missing series.

**Note on this entry's record (Cycle-59 wrap-up).** The R-039 screen's *reasoning* was written in the Cycle-59 cycle log, but the screen's **decision line and its known-baseline row were never written** — so for one cycle the rejection log asserted a screen whose family was absent from the rediscovery guard. Both are now written. **No verdict, no gap closure and no score changed.**


### R-040 — Published fee or toll that prices a third party's dated flow (template C) — bounded negative (Cycle 60)

**Family screened (state-first):** A published fee or toll schedule that prices a third party's dated flow (clearing/processing, registration/tender, depositary/conversion, etc.). Screened against literature with strict bar (published decision-time state, sign-determining not just magnitude, unclaimed sign, no published baseline, feasible clean test; Cycle-52 third test).

**Reasons (independent):**
1. **Published baseline/cost, not an unclaimed sign-determiner:** Fee schedules are widely published as costs; the literature treats them as frictions already embedded in arbitrage/flow calculations.
2. **Fails G-12 (sign/existence):** Fee level alone changes magnitude, not sign/existence of a known published effect in an unclaimed way; no instance where a published fee threshold clearly flips existence of a known effect without the effect/sign already being claimed was identified.
3. **No clean continuously published decision-time state with unclaimed variation:** Any state-dependent variation in fees that would matter is not clearly published as a decision-time state for a general family; available fee data is typically schedule-level, not a continuously varying decision-time state that meets the bar.
4. **Anticipation/competition:** Published fees are known in advance and competed away; the comparison is standard.
5. **No feasible clean test with unclaimed mechanism:** No candidate mechanism passes the strict bar.

**Dated re-screen condition:** Re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time fee/toll schedule shows a threshold that varies by published state (not fixed schedule alone) with documented cadence/coverage, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-040).** The published-fee-or-toll family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 69**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no `S-` identifier was consumed; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because reasons are independent of data availability in the sense that no unclaimed sign-determining instance was found and the form fails G-12.

---

## R-041 — Published count or queue state at decision date (template D) — bounded negative

**Family screened.** Published count or queue state at decision date: backlog, pending-registration, outstanding-notional/capacity, outstanding orders/pipeline. First in-depth screen of template (D).

**Screened state-first against the register's literature base.** Published counts/queues (exchange order-book depth, regulator pending-registration counts, outstanding-notional/capacity, pipeline/backlog) exist in many markets, but the published count at a decision date is a baseline *level* already embedded in published baselines; to change sign/existence (G-12) rather than just size, an unclaimed sign-determining instance would need a published threshold that flips whether the action is worthwhile, with the count published *before* the decision and not already claimed. Strict bar not met for a general family: (i) published counts are baseline levels, widely known and already embedded in price formation; (ii) the count alone does not constitute an unclaimed sign-determiner (fails G-12); (iii) decision-time *variation* that would flip existence is not clearly published as a continuous, decision-time state at the granularity needed (G-13 trap risk, cf. EU ETS TNAC-style late/publication); (iv) any claimed threshold is anticipated or already treated as a friction/baseline; (v) strict bar not satisfied (no unclaimed sign-determining state meeting Cycle-52 third test for a general family).

**Dated re-screen condition:** Re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time count/queue state shows a threshold that varies by published state with documented cadence/coverage, published *before* the decision, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-041).** The published-count/queue-state family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 70**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no `S-` identifier was consumed; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**.

---

## R-042 — Published reserve/availability/headroom state at decision date (template E) — bounded negative

**Family screened.** Published reserve/availability/headroom state at decision date: margin cushion/buffer, available capacity/headroom, position-limit headroom, inventory availability. Screened against literature with strict bar (published decision-time state, sign-determining not just magnitude, unclaimed sign, no published baseline, feasible clean test; Cycle-52 third test).

**Reasons (independent):**
1. **Published baseline levels, not unclaimed sign-determiners:** Reserve/headroom levels are widely published as baseline indicators; the literature treats them as state variables that condition effects (e.g. margin constraints) rather than as unclaimed edges where the count/level itself produces a new, unclaimed sign-determining effect distinct from published baselines.
2. **Fails G-12 (sign/existence):** Conditioning on reserve/availability changes magnitude in published work more often than it changes the sign or existence of an effect in an unclaimed way. No instance was identified where a published reserve/headroom threshold clearly flips existence of a known effect without the sign already being claimed.
3. **State form / G-13 risk:** Many reserve/availability measures are either fixed schedule-level figures or published with a lag relative to the decision; to be usable as decision-time state the measure must be published before the decision at the needed cadence. Continuously published, decision-time headroom meeting the bar is uncommon as a general template.
4. **Anticipation/competition:** Where reserve/headroom is public and widely observed, market participants anticipate binding thresholds; effects are competed away or already embedded in prices.
5. **No feasible clean test with unclaimed mechanism:** No candidate mechanism passes the strict bar for this family.

**Dated re-screen condition:** Re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time reserve/availability/headroom state shows a threshold that varies by published state with documented cadence/coverage, published *before* the decision, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-042).** The published-reserve/availability/headroom family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 71**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no `S-` identifier was consumed; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**.

---

## R-043 — Published constraint breach / non-compliance events at decision date (template F) — bounded negative

**Family screened.** Published constraint breach / non-compliance events at decision date: margin violations, position-limit breaches, enforcement actions, covenant breaches, and other contractually published states in which a party is in breach of a dated obligation. Screened state-first against literature with strict bar (published decision-time state, sign-determining not just magnitude, unclaimed sign, no published baseline, feasible clean test; Cycle-52 third test). **This is the register's first screen of template (F).**

**Screened state-first against the register's literature base.** Published breach/non-compliance events exist as institutional event series in many markets (exchange enforcement actions, regulator covenant-breach disclosures, margin-violation notices, position-limit breach reports). However, the screen failed the strict bar for a general family on five independent grounds:

1. **Event publication is a third-party disclosure obligation, not a constrained flow conceding an edge.** An enforcement action or breach disclosure is itself price-moving information released by the regulator or exchange; the screen does not extract a concession from a constrained third party, so there is no S-151 analogue to harvest.

2. **Fails G-12 (sign/existence).** A published breach event is a fact of non-compliance, not an unclaimed sign-determining state that flips existence of a known published effect. Conditioning on breach history changes position sizing or risk parameters (magnitude) rather than changing whether an effect exists or its direction, in any unclaimed way identified by the screen.

3. **Lagged, security-specific, already embedded in price formation.** Breach/enforcement disclosures are typically published after the decision-relevant window has closed, are security- or issuer-specific rather than a continuous cross-sectional state, and are already reflected in prices at publication. The decision-time state a candidate would need is not cleanly published as a continuous series before the decision.

4. **Effective sample is the number of breaches, not securities × dates.** Breach events are rare per issuer; any candidate family built on them has a small effective sample, which the register treats as a binding constraint under its state-first discipline.

5. **Anticipation and competition.** Where breach likelihood is public (e.g. CDS spreads, borrow fees, position-limit proximity disclosures), market participants price it in advance; any residual is competed away or already embedded in published baselines.

**Dated re-screen condition:** Re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time breach/non-compliance event series shows a threshold that varies by published state with documented cadence/coverage, published *before* the decision, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-043).** The published constraint breach / non-compliance events family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 72**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no `S-` identifier was consumed; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of data availability — no unclaimed sign-determining instance meeting G-12 was found for this family.

## R-044 — Published constraint waiver/amendment events at decision date (template G) — bounded negative

**Family screened.** Published constraint waiver/amendment events at decision date: events in which a dated constraint is waived, amended, relaxed or re-negotiated and the waiver/amendment is published (covenant waivers, position-limit amendments, margin-requirement amendments, regulatory forbearance notices, contractually published renegotiation outcomes). Screened state-first against literature with strict bar (published decision-time state, sign-determining not just magnitude, unclaimed sign, no published baseline, feasible clean test; Cycle-52 third test). **This is the register's first screen of template (G).**

**Screened state-first against the register's literature base.** Published waiver/amendment events exist as institutional event series (exchange rule filings, regulator forbearance notices, covenant-waiver disclosures, amended margin schedules). However, the screen failed the strict bar for a general family on five independent grounds:

1. **Event publication is a third-party disclosure obligation, not a constrained flow conceding an edge.** A waiver or amendment is itself information released by the constraining party; the screen does not extract a concession from a constrained third party, so there is no S-151 analogue to harvest.

2. **Fails G-12 (sign/existence).** A published waiver/amendment is a fact of relaxed constraint, not an unclaimed sign-determining state that flips existence of a known published effect. Conditioning on waiver history changes terms or sizing (magnitude) rather than changing whether an effect exists or its direction, in any unclaimed way identified by the screen.

3. **Lagged, security-specific, already embedded in price formation.** Waiver/amendment disclosures are typically published after the decision-relevant window has closed, are issuer- or security-specific rather than a continuous cross-sectional state, and are already reflected in prices at publication. The decision-time state a candidate would need is not cleanly published as a continuous series before the decision.

4. **Effective sample is the number of waivers/amendments, not securities × dates.** Waiver events are rare per issuer; any candidate family built on them has a small effective sample, which the register treats as a binding constraint under its state-first discipline.

5. **Anticipation and competition.** Where waiver likelihood is public (e.g. CDS spreads, borrow fees, covenant proximity disclosures), market participants price it in advance; any residual is competed away or already embedded in published baselines.

**Dated re-screen condition:** Re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time waiver/amendment event series shows a threshold that varies by published state with documented cadence/coverage, published *before* the decision, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-044).** The published constraint waiver/amendment events family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 73**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no `S-` identifier was consumed; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of data availability — no unclaimed sign-determining instance meeting G-12 was found for this family.

---

## R-045 — Published regulatory change-of-law state at decision date (template H) — bounded negative

**Family screened (state-first):** A published change of law or regulation — statute, regulation, or exchange/clearinghouse rule — with a published effective date that alters an obligation or constraint applicable to a participant class, where the change is published before the decision date. Screened against literature with strict bar (published decision-time state, sign-determining not just magnitude, unclaimed sign, no published baseline, feasible clean test; Cycle-52 third test). **This is the register's first screen of template (H).**

**Reasons (independent):**

1. **Published in advance, so anticipated.** A change of law is enacted and published with an effective date (legislative passage, notice-and-comment, rule adoption) well before it binds, so any flow it creates is anticipated by the market before the decision date. G-13 passes for availability but that is the defect: there is no unclaimed informational asymmetry and any reaction is priced in anticipation.

2. **Fails G-12 (sign/existence).** A change of law changes the terms, coverage or cost of an obligation; it changes magnitude far more often than it changes the sign or existence of a known published effect in an unclaimed way. No instance was identified where a published change of law flips the existence of a known effect without the sign already being claimed.

3. **The constrained party is the obligor (S-151 analogue).** The party bound by the new law adjusts for its own account; no third party is forced to trade at a concession that another participant can harvest, so there is no S-151 analogue to extract.

4. **Effective sample is small.** Changes of law are discrete, dated, infrequent events — a handful of episodes per jurisdiction, not securities × dates — so a positive rule would have almost no degrees of freedom.

5. **Anticipation and competition wash out the residual.** Where the change is public and widely observed, participants pre-position; any residual is competed away or already embedded in published baselines.

**Dated re-screen condition:** Re-screen only if a change of law creates a dated, price-insensitive, non-discretionary flow executed by a participant other than the obligor, with a **published list of affected securities** and an **unclaimed sign** that the literature shows is sign-determining and not already claimed.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-045).** The published regulatory change-of-law state family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 74**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no `S-` identifier was consumed; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of data availability — no unclaimed sign-determining instance meeting G-12 was found for this family.

---

## R-046 — Published clamp/cap band of a continuously published state at decision date (template I) — bounded negative

**Family screened (state-first):** A published **clamp, cap, floor or band** — the parameter that bounds a
continuously published state, so that the state is *uninformative by construction* over a published interval — where
the bound is published by the venue or issuer before the decision date and is observable in real time. The family is
genuinely new in kind relative to templates A–H: those were states about *constraints, obligations, prices, counts,
reserves, breaches, waivers and changes of law*, whereas this is a **published bound on a state's own functional
form**. The instance that surfaced the family is the perpetual-futures funding clamp and cap (S-086's Binance
clamp parameters; S-321's dYdX eight-hour cap of 600 % × (initial margin − maintenance margin) and its
governance-adjustable clamp factor; the published clamp analyses in S-317 and S-318). Screened state-first with
**G-12 first**, against the strict bar (published decision-time state, sign-determining not just magnitude, unclaimed
sign, no published baseline, feasible clean test; Cycle-52 third test). **This is the register's first screen of
template (I).**

**Reasons (independent):**

1. **The band is a published function parameter, so its crossing is a deterministic function of an already-published
   price.** A clamp binds when the premium or gap crosses a published threshold; the premium is itself a
   continuously published, zero-lag state. Conditioning on "the band has been crossed" therefore adds **no
   information beyond that published price** — it re-describes the state's level rather than supplying a new state.
   The register's own state-first rule requires a candidate to condition on a state that is not a by-product of a
   state already published, and a bound on that price fails it.

2. **Fails G-12 (sign/existence) as an edge.** A published bound changes the *magnitude and tail distribution* of a
   state, not the sign or the existence of an unclaimed effect. The published work treats the clamp as an **inaction
   region** in which the state carries no information about the crowd: persistent futures-spot discrepancies cannot
   be explained by transaction fees alone and the clamping function is a key driver (S-317); the futures-to-spot
   ratio is **bimodal at higher frequencies because of the inaction region**, and clamping strength relates to
   funding-rate volatility **non-monotonically** (S-318). None of that creates an unclaimed sign — it creates a
   published characterisation of a state's distribution.

3. **The band's real use is as a filter, not an edge — and that role is already published and already used.** A
   published bound used to *clean* another candidate's state is a baseline, not a discovery. That is precisely how
   the register now uses it: **V-008's Cycle-66 amendment** requires IDEA-009's percentile state to be computed on
   **clamp-excluded** observations and requires the venue's published clamp and cap to be recorded per venue (S-086,
   S-317, S-318, S-321). A state kind that earns its place as a **modifier of another candidate's state** is a
   contribution to that candidate, and is recorded as such — it is not an edge in its own right.

4. **The parameter is governance-adjustable and venue-specific, so the state is not stable.** dYdX's own published
   documentation states that governance can adjust the **funding-rate clamp factor**, the premium-vote clamp factor,
   the minimum number of votes per premium sample, the epoch/funding interval and the liquidity tier (S-321), and
   the clamp and cap structure is an implementation detail that differs by venue (S-086, S-321). A rule keyed to a
   band is therefore not portable across venues without restatement and can change without a market event — the
   same instability the register already recorded for the funding cadence in IDEA-009's own timing hazards.

5. **The effective sample is the number of band exits, and the published work already uses those episodes.** A clamp
   exit is episodic, not a cross-sectional state; the published empirical work on this family is already conditioned
   on such episodes (S-310's 274 extreme events; S-311's 1,696 events; S-319's 19 contracts on an 8-hour cadence).
   A candidate built on band exits would compete for the same small sample against work that has already used it,
   and it would have to beat a published result rather than merely differ from one.

**Dated re-screen condition:** Re-screen only if a continuously published state has a published bound that changes
the **sign or the existence** of an effect (not merely its magnitude), where the bound is **not** a deterministic
function of an already-published price, where the parameter is stable or its changes are themselves published in
advance with a dated effect, and where the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect conditioned on the
*bound* rather than on the state it bounds.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-046).** The published clamp/cap-band family is recorded in the known-baseline
table (**row 75**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; **no `S-` identifier was consumed by
the screen itself** — the sources it cites (S-317, S-318, S-321, S-086) were registered earlier in the same cycle by
the IDEA-009 prior-art re-read; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**,
because the reasons are independent of data availability — the family failed on the *sign-and-existence* limb
(reason 2) and on the *already-published-state* limb (reason 1), not on a missing series. A **second** known-baseline
row (**row 76**) was added in the same cycle for the **crypto perpetual funding-extreme reversal family**, so that
the rediscovery guard records the published conditional result that IDEA-009 is now an incremental variant of.

## R-047 — Reporting-date balance-sheet window dressing into public safe assets (mechanism-first family) — bounded negative

**What was screened.** The register's first **mechanism-first** family screen since Cycle 13: a family defined
by an **institutional mechanism** rather than by a published state. The mechanism is a **statutory or
regulatory reporting-date balance-sheet obligation** that forces a participant to rebalance a dated, largely
price-insensitive flow: at a reporting date, non-US banks reduce their borrowing from US money market funds
because of reporting and regulatory considerations, the money-market funds, constrained to hold short-term
high-quality assets, redeploy the displaced cash into Treasury bills, and short-maturity US Treasury yields
fall. The same institutional shape appears in the Treasury auction channel, where dealer balance sheets absorb
inventory risk around auction dates.

**Why the family was worth screening now.** The register's *state-kind* screen programme (templates A–I) has
been recorded as exhausted for candidate production since Cycle 66, and the Cycle-73 handoff named a
**mechanism-first search** as the fallback once the P-011 check was exhausted — which it was, in Cycle 73. A
mechanism-first screen is a *different* operation from a state-kind screen and had not been run since Cycle 13.

**Sources.** **S-329** (Sun, J. and Tian, Y. 2026, "When Banks Retreat: The Demand for Public Safe Assets",
SSRN working paper 6913784, posted 3 July 2026, last revised 15 October 2026; earlier title "Private Money,
Public Debt: Quarter-End Price Pressure in Treasury Bills"; abstract **index-rendered** — the SSRN landing page
returned HTTP 403 to a direct fetch) and **S-330** (Fleming, M. J., Liu, W. and Nguyen, G. 2026, "Intraday
Price Pressure and Order Flow Around U.S. Treasury Auctions", Federal Reserve Bank of New York Staff Report
1188, March 2026, revised July 2026, DOI 10.59576/sr.1188; **line-verified** from the staff-report page).

**Reason 1 — the sign and the window are already published, so the family's headline is claimed.** The
published result states the mechanism, the window and the sign together: "quarter-end bank window dressing
shifts institutional demand from private to public safe assets. Around regulatory reporting dates, Non-U.S.
banks reduce borrowing from U.S. money market funds; constrained to hold short-term, high-quality assets, the
funds redeploy the displaced cash into Treasury bills. As a result, short-maturity U.S. Treasury yields fall
by 3–9 basis points at quarter-ends." A candidate in this family would be re-presenting a published effect,
which the register treats as a baseline, not as a discovery.

**Reason 2 — the state that determines the *size* of the effect is not published at decision time.** The
redeployment is generated by **non-US banks' private reporting-date balance-sheet positions**, which no outside
party can observe; the observable proxies are money-market-fund portfolio holdings, published on the
**N-MFP monthly cycle with a two-month lag**, and the dealer balance-sheet capacity that conditions the
auction channel, published **quarterly and lagged** (S-330's own conditioner: "This pressure strengthens when
dealers face tighter risk-bearing constraints"). So a state-conditioned instance of this family fails the
register's decision-time-state bar (G-13) and the Cycle-52 third test — the state may not be a public
real-time by-product of the constrained flow's own trading.

**Reason 3 — the published elasticity is a size statement, not a sign statement, so the family fails G-12 on
the binding limb.** The published estimate is that "money market fund demand inflows equal to 1 percent of
Treasury bill market value lower yield spreads by 4.9 basis points". That is a *magnitude* relation, and the
register's screen bar requires a published state that changes the **sign or the existence** of an effect, not
merely its size. The 3–9 basis-point quarter-end move is likewise a size, and the sign is fixed in the
published work.

**Reason 4 — the family adds no new *kind* of obligation to the register's existing baseline set.** The
register already holds the flow-concession template in several forms: liquidity provision around predictable
large trades, sovereign issuance calendars, the EU-ETS surrender-deadline concession (IDEA-013) and the US
Renewable Fuel Standard compliance-deadline concession (IDEA-014). The reporting-date instance is again a
dated, non-discretionary, largely price-insensitive flow whose obligor is not the party that benefits — the
S-151 analogue, already a standing reason in this log — and it fails the same limb for the same reason.

**Reason 5 — the published estimate is not reproducible or contestable from public data, and the effect is
framed by its authors as a policy quantity.** The published design is a "network shift-share design that
exploits predetermined fund–bank relationships", and those relationships are **not public data**, so an
outside party can neither re-run nor contest the estimate; and the paper's own use of the estimates is
"to quantify the additional public safe asset provision needed to stabilize Treasury bill yields at
quarter-ends and to inform policy recommendations for the existing ON RRP facility" — a market-clearing and
policy framing, not an unclaimed tradable edge. A candidate that cannot identify its own effect is not a
candidate.

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (a) a **published, decision-time** state determines the
**sign or the existence** (not merely the magnitude) of a reporting-date or auction-window concession in a
market where the flow is not already claimed; (b) that state is **not** a public real-time by-product of the
flow's own trading and **not** a deterministic function of an already-published price; (c) the published
magnitude is materially larger than the 3–9 basis points documented, or the effect is shown to persist after
the concession is competed away; and (d) the literature shows an **unclaimed** sign-determining effect
conditioned on the state rather than on the flow it produces.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-047).** The reporting-date window-dressing family is recorded in the
known-baseline table (**rows 79 and 80**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; the
screen registered its own sources (**S-329**, **S-330**) and consumed no further identifier; no score changed;
no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of
data availability — the family failed on the *already-published-effect* limb (reason 1) and on the
*sign-and-existence* limb (reason 3), not on a missing series. The screen's *product* is a **rediscovery
guard** (rows 79–82) and a **registered precedent** that the *mechanism-first* operation is distinct from the
exhausted *state-kind* screen programme and remains available to future cycles.

## R-048 — Fixed income cash mechanism families (Treasury auction price pressure, cash-futures basis trade, quarter-end window dressing, repo specialness) — bounded negative

**What was screened.** The register's second **mechanism-first** family screen (after R-047, Cycle 75): families defined by an **institutional mechanism in fixed income cash** rather than by a published state. Fixed income cash — U.S. Treasury cash, repo, futures and the auction cycle — was screened because it is the obvious gap now that IDEA-010 (corporate-bond ETF) is parked and the register holds no fixed-income candidate. Four published instances carry the family's shape:
- the Treasury auction cycle, where dealer balance sheets absorb inventory risk around auction dates and yields rise before an auction and reverse afterward (S-330, Fleming, Liu & Nguyen 2026, NY Fed Staff Report 1188, line-verified; Kopchak 2014, *Review of Quantitative Finance and Accounting* 43(1): 21–44);
- the Treasury cash-futures basis trade, a leveraged convergence arbitrage between a cash Treasury and its futures contract, financed in repo (Barth & Kahn, OFR Working Paper 21-01, April 2021; Glicoes, Iorio, Monin & Petrasek, Fed Notes 2024-03-08; CFTC MRAC report, 2024);
- quarter-end bank window dressing shifting money-market-fund demand into Treasury bills (R-047's instance, S-329 index-rendered SSRN abstract);
- repo specialness and the on-the-run premium, priced through the special-collateral repo rate (D'Amico & Pancost 2018, FEDS WP 2018-21).

**Why the family was worth screening now.** The state-kind screen programme (templates A–I) is recorded as exhausted for candidate production since Cycle 66; a *mechanism-first* screen is the distinct operation that remains legitimate. Fixed income cash is the register's largest uncovered venue and the obvious gap after IDEA-010's parking removed the corporate-bond-ETF leg.

**Sources.** **S-330** (line-verified from the NY Fed staff-report page). Barth & Kahn (OFR wp 21-01), Glicoes et al. (Fed Notes 2024-03-08), the CFTC MRAC report, Kotomin & Winters (quarter-end window dressing) and D'Amico & Pancost (FEDS WP 2018-21) are cited from their published abstracts / institutional pages; none was fetched as a binary document in this cycle, so no body text is quoted and their wording is recorded as published-abstract / institutional-page only, not line-verified.

**Reason 1 — the sign, the window and the magnitude are already published, so each instance's headline is claimed.** S-330 documents "yields rise in the hours before an auction and reverse afterward", sized at 0.7–1.2 bps intraday and 2–5 bps over ten days, strengthening when dealer risk-bearing capacity is tight. The basis trade's convergence, its leverage and its March 2020 unwind are published institutional record. Window dressing's 3–9 bps quarter-end yield move and the 4.9 bps per 1 percent-of-market-value inflow elasticity are published (R-047). Repo specialness's on-the-run premium and its auction-cycle seasonality are published. A candidate in any of these instances would be re-presenting a published effect — a baseline, not a discovery.

**Reason 2 — the state determining the *size* of the effect is not published at decision time.** Dealer risk-bearing capacity (S-330's conditioner) is quarterly and lagged; money-market-fund holdings publish on the N-MFP monthly cycle with a two-month lag; repo specialness is episodic and specialised rather than a continuous decision-time series. Each fails the decision-time-state bar (G-13) and the Cycle-52 third test — the state may not be a public real-time by-product of the constrained flow's own trading.

**Reason 3 — the published elasticity is a size statement, not a sign statement, so the family fails G-12 on the binding limb.** Every instance reports a magnitude (basis points, elasticity, premium size) with a fixed sign in the published work; none offers a published, decision-time state that changes the **sign or the existence** of an unclaimed effect.

**Reason 4 — the family adds no new *kind* of obligation to the register's existing baseline set.** The register already holds dated, non-discretionary, largely price-insensitive flow obligations in several forms: the EU ETS surrender-deadline concession (IDEA-013), the US RFS compliance-deadline concession (IDEA-014), reconstitution anticipation and tender offers. The fixed income cash instances are again dated obligations whose obligor is not the party that benefits — the S-151 analogue, already a standing reason in this log — and they fail the same limb for the same reason.

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (a) a **published, decision-time** state determines the **sign or the existence** (not merely the magnitude) of a fixed income cash concession in a market where the flow is not already claimed; (b) that state is **not** a public real-time by-product of the flow's own trading and **not** a deterministic function of an already-published price; and (c) the literature shows an **unclaimed** sign-determining effect conditioned on the state rather than on the flow it produces.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-048).** The fixed income cash mechanism family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 85**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; S-330 was already registered (line-verified); the other cited sources were not fetched as binary documents and consume no `S-` identifier; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of data availability — the family failed on the *already-published-effect* limb (reason 1) and the *sign-and-existence* limb (reason 3), not on a missing series. The screen's product is a **rediscovery guard** (row 85) and a second registered precedent that the *mechanism-first* operation is distinct from the exhausted *state-kind* screen programme.

## R-049 — Weather-derivative contract pricing: the HDD/CDD temperature-premium family (futures, options and seasonal strips) — bounded negative

**What was screened.** The register's third **mechanism-first** family screen (after R-047 in Cycle 75 and R-048 in Cycle 80): exchange-traded contracts written on a city's **heating- and cooling-degree-day (HDD/CDD) index** — the CME's temperature futures, the options on them and the seasonal strips — together with the premium that the market's price structure embeds. The family's shape is a **risk premium paid by the hedging side to the liquidity-supplying side**: energy and utility companies buy and short the local temperature futures as hedgers, financial institutions supply the liquidity, and the contract price embeds a compensation for bearing the weather risk.

**Why the family was worth screening now.** The state-kind screen programme (templates A–I) is recorded as exhausted for candidate production since Cycle 66, and the mechanism-first families R-039 … R-048 are all recorded in the known-baseline table. The weather-derivative family was **absent from the known-baseline table** and from every earlier screen, and the register's standing instruction is a **venue** instruction rather than another state variable in listed US equities. Two qualifications were recorded before the screen ran: the shortlist's Cycle-31 retrospective note claims an "energy/weather" family screen that **no `R-` entry, no cycle-log block and no baseline row attests** (so the family is treated as **unscreened** — an unwritten artifact is not a result), and the register's own scope discipline forbids a screen that pads the record, so the screen was run against a **pre-registered decision rule** with five independent grounds, of which the *existence* of a published, decision-time, sign-determining conditioner was the decisive one.

**Sources.** **S-332** (Weagley 2019, *The Review of Financial Studies* 32(6): 2456–2497, DOI 10.1093/rfs/hhy098 — Crossref-verified metadata, **line-verified from the working-paper version**, and in **Cycle 88 upgraded** to a *published-version* basis for the abstract and the Internet Appendix (S-349); the published article's **body** is paywalled and no passage from it is quoted); **S-333** (Bae, Jacobs, Jeon, Szaura & Zurita, "Weather Volatility Risk Premium", SSRN 4572123 — Crossref-verified record, **line-verified from the 29 August 2024 conference version**; a working paper, not peer-reviewed); **S-334** (Weagley 2014, *Essays on the Weather Derivatives Market*, University of Michigan dissertation, Deep Blue handle 2027.42/108993 — **line-verified**); **S-335** (Chincarini 2011, *Journal of Futures Markets* 31(1): 1–33, DOI 10.1002/fut.20463 — **line-verified in Cycle 88**; the Cycle-87 text-extraction failure was an *extractor* failure, not a document defect, and the published body is now extracted and quoted); **S-336** (FORRS 2024 factsheet, *Energy Market and Weather Derivatives* — **line-verified**, used for venue context only). **The venue's own pages returned HTTP 403 on every route in Cycle 87**, so the venue's *current* listing status was recorded there as **unverified**; **in Cycle 88 that item was closed** — see the dated re-screen condition below and S-344 … S-348.

**Reason 1 — the family's sign is already published, so the headline is claimed.** Contracts are "priced near their actuarially fair value" in normal times; in the financial crisis "contract prices one-month before maturity fall by 2.2%", "equivalent to an annualized weather risk premium of 26%", with the declines "greatest for high margin and high total risk contracts" and larger when the TED spread and the VIX rise (S-332, from the line-verified working-paper version; the dissertation lineage reports 30 % per year in the crisis, S-334). The **option leg** is published as well: "implied volatilities of weather derivatives are substantially greater than the realized volatilities for all cities in our sample", a weather variance risk premium of "0.29 on average with standard deviation of 0.22" (S-333). A candidate in this family would be re-presenting a published effect — a baseline, not a discovery.

**Reason 2 — what survives the published sign is a *magnitude*, and the conditioner of that magnitude is not published at decision time.** The published conditioner of the *futures* premium is **financial-sector stress** — the TED spread and the VIX (S-332) — a published macro series, so the sign-changing state is already in the literature and the unclaimed remainder is the size of a premium, not the existence of one. The published conditioner of the *option leg* is the local credit-spread and equity-variance cross-section (S-333), whose own question is explicitly **not** whether the premium is tradable. No published, decision-time state in this family determines the **sign or the existence** of an unclaimed effect, which is the register's standing test (G-12) and the register's Cycle-52 third test.

**Reason 3 — the published record contests the premise.** The weather-derivatives market is characterised in the peer-reviewed record as **efficient despite its illiquidity**, with temperature-forward premia reported as statistically insignificant and sign-flipping (S-335). **Cycle 88 strengthened this reason with the published article's own wording, now line-verified:** the published abstract states that "generally the market seems very efficient despite its lack of liquidity" and that "risk premia that seem to vary across cities and over time", and the published results report the HDD premium at "0.47% and insignificant for the most liquid cities from 1999 to 2004" against a CDD premium of "5.76% and significantly different than zero", with the **sign flipping** between the two sub-periods — "the CDD average premia for the top five cities actually changes sign from the 1999–2004 period to the 2005–2008 period" — and the published conclusion that "one fails to reject the hypothesis that this market trades very efficiently". The register's rule that an index-rendered rendering may not serve as a verification basis is now satisfied for this entry as well, since the body was extracted and quoted in Cycle 88. A future candidate in this family would therefore have to answer a *published null*, not merely an absence of published work.

**Reason 4 — the cost floor and the venue's own structure bind.** The venue is "relatively illiquid", "Bid-ask spreads are large and many deals are conducted off exchange and submitted as block trades", markets "typically open in the 1-3 weeks before the contract month" (the median market is opened 39 days before maturity) and "participants rarely change their positions" — after a market is initially opened, "open interest does not change on 85% of trading days" (S-332, S-334). A spread-and-impact floor of that shape, in a market where the participant's position is set by a hedge that runs to the contract month, is a cost that a premium of the published size (statistically insignificant in normal times) does not clear. **The venue is not dead** — average open interest for January–September 2023 was four times 2022's and twelve times 2019's (S-336) — so this is a cost-and-structure ground, not an extinction ground.

**Reason 5 — the effective sample and the regime dependence are adverse.** The field's own published sample **discards every quote with zero or missing open interest** (S-333), so the quotes that define the premium are the quotes a strategy would actually have had to trade; the published sample is a **1999–2008/09** window, and the largest published premium is a **financial-crisis** phenomenon, so it is a *regime* rather than a standing condition; and the "contract prices one-month before maturity" claim is itself a statement about a narrow, pre-expiry window in which the hedge is being unwound. A candidate here would be claiming a size in a regime that a decision-time rule cannot identify in advance.

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (a) a **published, decision-time** state is shown to determine the **sign or the existence** (not merely the magnitude) of a temperature premium in a market where the premium is not already claimed; (b) that state is **not** a public real-time by-product of the participants' own trading and **not** a deterministic function of an already-published price; (c) the published record's **null** (S-335, and the normal-time null in S-332/S-334) is answered rather than assumed away; and (d) the venue's **current listing status, contract specifications and settlement basis** are verified from a document that can be fetched and text-extracted — the CME pages returned **HTTP 403** on every route in the Cycle-87 screen, so that item was recorded there as **unverified, not closed**. **Cycle 88 update — condition (d) is now CLOSED, and the other three are untouched.** The venue's current listing status, contract specifications and settlement basis are verified from four venue documents and one regulator-hosted rulebook copy (S-344 — the venue's fact card, "18 cities around the world – 13 in the U.S., four in Europe, and one in Asia", with the settlement arithmetic at a "$20" tick; S-345 — the venue's settlement procedure, "settled at 15:15 CT daily", the "in period" fallback that adds "the actual observed heating or cooling degree days to date to the 10-year average", cash settlement on the MDA EarthSat index and NCDC data for U.S. contracts; S-346 — the venue's own CFTC rule filing of 4 May 2023, "Initial Listing of Twenty-Four (24) U.S. (Boston, Burbank, Houston and Philadelphia) Weather Futures Contracts", effective for trade date 22 May 2023; S-347 — the **regulator-hosted** text of Rulebook Chapters 403 and 405, the trading unit "U.S. dollars $20 times the Index", the one-index-point minimum fluctuation, the termination of trading and the final settlement by the MDA Information Systems index; and S-348 — the venue's **live** Weather Products page, rendered 20 October 2026, still carrying the complex, its futures and options tabs, its named execution channels and its weather brokers). **Closing condition (d) does not re-open the family**: conditions (a) and (c) remain unfulfilled, and the screen's verdict is unchanged.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-049).** The weather-derivative contract-pricing family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 86**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; **five `S-` identifiers were consumed (S-332 … S-336)**; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of data availability — the family failed on the *already-published-sign* limb (reason 1), the *sign-and-existence* limb (reason 2), the *contested-premise* limb (reason 3), the *cost-and-venue-structure* limb (reason 4) and the *sample-and-regime* limb (reason 5). The screen's product is a **rediscovery guard** (row 86) and a third registered precedent that the *mechanism-first* operation is distinct from the exhausted *state-kind* screen programme and can still be run on a family that is genuinely new.

## R-050 — Closed-end-fund / BDC traded price-to-NAV discount family (listed closed-end funds and listed business development companies) — bounded negative

**What was screened.** The register's fourth **mechanism-first** family screen (after R-047 in Cycle 75, R-048 in Cycle 80 and R-049 in Cycle 87): **listed vehicles that issue a fixed share count, offer no continuous creation/redemption, and therefore trade at a persistent discount or premium to a published net asset value** — the listed **closed-end funds** and the listed **business development companies (BDCs)**. The family's shape is a **structural, permanent wedge** between a vehicle's market price and the value of its portfolio: the price of the wrapper is set in a secondary market with no creation or redemption mechanism, so the discount (or premium) is the price of a bundle of services — liquidity transformation, a fee load, a distribution obligation and a tax overhang — rather than a mispricing that a mechanism should remove.

**Why the family was worth screening now.** The family was **absent from the known-baseline table**: no row covers closed-end funds, business development companies, price-to-NAV discounts, or the closed-end fund "puzzle", and the whole file contains no mention of a closed-end fund or a BDC outside the register's own new material. It is also a **neighbour, not a re-presentation** of anything already screened: the baseline's ETF rows concern vehicles that *do* have a creation/redemption mechanism (row 84's "Fractional baskets and persistent ETF–NAV discrepancies" and the AP-as-buffer account) and the residual of the parked IDEA-010 is precisely the *unmeasurable* part of an ETF–NAV discount; a **closed-end** vehicle's discount is a different mechanism, with the opposite structure — the discount is *permanent by design* rather than a mechanical widening. The screen was run against a **pre-registered decision rule** with five independent grounds, and the register's own scope discipline — a padded screen is worse than no screen — was applied: the screen was stopped as soon as five independent grounds were established, and **no source was cited that was not fetched and text-extracted**.

**Sources.** **S-337** (Cherkes, Sagi & Stanton, "A Liquidity-Based Theory of Closed-End Funds" — 2007 working-paper version, **line-verified**); **S-338** (Chen & Tour, "The Discount Delusion: The Swedish Closed-End Fund Premium Puzzle" — 2013 student working paper, **line-verified**; used for the *published negative result on the trade*, with its non-peer-reviewed status and its Swedish venue stated); **S-339** (Davydiuk, Marchuk & Rosen, "Market Discipline in the Direct Lending Space", *The Review of Financial Studies* 37(4): 1190–1264 — **line-verified from the published version** in the NSF public-access repository); **S-340** (Suhonen, "Direct Lending Returns", *Financial Analysts Journal* 80(1): 57–83 — **line-verified from the publisher's version of record**, obtained from the author's institutional repository after the publisher's own routes failed); **S-341** (Fenaroli, "The Closed-End Fund Puzzle: New Evidence From Business Development Companies", Harvard College undergraduate thesis 2019 — **line-verified**, used only as a *secondary* magnitude source and never as the primary basis for a reason); **S-342** (15 U.S.C. § 80a-60 — the statutory BDC below-NAV issuance and repurchase-offer conditions, **line-verified** from the Cornell LII); **S-343** (17 CFR 270.23c-1 — the closed-end company's own repurchase conditions, **line-verified** from eCFR). **Malkiel & Xu, "The Persistence and Predictability of Closed-End Fund Discounts", was not obtained** — the Wharton host times out, the CiteSeerX route refuses the connection and the NBER working-paper number resolves to a different paper — and is therefore recorded as an **index-rendered neighbour only** and is **not** used as a verification basis for any reason below.

**Reason 1 — the family's headline is published, and the magnitude is published too.** A closed-end fund "usually trade[s] at a discount to NAV" (S-337), and the discount is not an unexplained anomaly but a **published, model-derived quantity**: it "results from a trade-off between the liquidity benefits of investing in the CEF and the fees charged by the fund's managers", with the model explaining "why funds are issued at a premium to net asset value (NAV), and why they later usually trade at a discount" (S-337). For the listed BDC the published magnitudes are specific: BDCs "tend to trade at a mid single-digit discount in the long run (x̄_MCW = −4.6%)" with a "mean premium standard deviation … relatively high (σ_i(x_i,t) = 11.9%)" (S-341, a secondary source), and at index level the market-value and NAV total returns differ by "8.63% per annum based on market values and 9.41% based on NAVs" (S-340). A candidate in this family would be re-presenting a published effect at a published magnitude — a baseline, not a discovery.

**Reason 2 — the sign-changing state is published, and the trade itself is published, with a published (negative) sign.** The state that moves the discount is in the literature: the broad BDC market tends "to generally trade close to the NAV, **except during times of market stress when the discount can widen substantially**—best illustrated by performance during the 2020 pandemic" (S-340), and the discount is **episodic and dated**: before the May 2014 index-exclusion event "BDCs' shares were traded at 5%–10% above their NAVs on average", and the exclusion "pushing BDCs' shares to be traded at a discount for **nearly 3 years** thereafter" (S-339), with the deepening concentrated in "the first six months of 2022" (S-340). The **trade** is published as well, and its published sign is **against** the buyer of the discount: "we find that **discounts are negatively correlated with future abnormal returns. Those who buy high discount CEFs thinking that they are bargains will have the numbers against them; the apparent undervaluation of the assets is only a delusion**" (S-338), and, in the listed-BDC cross-section, "BDCs with **greater price-to-NAV premium (or smaller discount) outperforming those with a smaller premium (larger discount) by all return metrics**" (S-340). So the family's sign is claimed *and* the residual "buy the discount" rule has already been tested in the published record, with a published sign and a published result. What survives is at most a magnitude inside a published effect.

**Reason 3 — the arbitrage is structurally blocked, and the blocking is published law, not an obstacle a candidate could clear.** The vehicle has **no creation or redemption mechanism**: "**Unlike other organizational forms, such as open-end funds (OEFs) and exchange-traded funds (ETFs), CEFs are not subject to large-scale creation or redemption of shares**" (S-337) — which is exactly why the discount can persist, and why a discount in this family is **not** the ETF-style mispricing that the register's parked IDEA-010 residual concerns. The vehicle's own balance sheet cannot close the discount either: a BDC "can access capital markets this way without any restrictions **only when their shares are traded at a premium to net asset value (NAV)**. For below-NAV issuances, BDCs need to seek an approval from their existing shareholders" (S-339), and a BDC "must distribute at least 90% of investment income to shareholders annually", which "severely restricts the companies' ability to accumulate retained profits" (S-340); a closed-end company's own repurchase is bounded by rule, requiring that "at least 90 percent of the net income of the issuer for the last preceding fiscal year … was distributed to its shareholders" and that "the purchase is made at a price not above the market value, if any, or the asset value of such security, whichever is lower" (S-343). And the family already contains a **published, statutory, quarterly repurchase mechanism at net asset value**: a BDC with shareholder approval to issue below NAV must give the approving shareholders "the opportunity (which may include a tender offer) to sell the securities held by that shareholder as of that applicable approval date, **with 25 percent of those securities to be repurchased in each of the 4 calendar quarters following** the calendar quarter in which that applicable approval date takes place" (S-342). A candidate here would be re-presenting a **published mechanism** — and the residual behind it is a *fee load* and a *tax overhang*, both of which are published parts of the discount, not unclaimed edges.

**Reason 4 — the cost floor binds, and the published result already says the trade does not clear it.** The "free lunch" reading of a closed-end fund discount is a **published framing with a published negative answer**: the apparent undervaluation "is only a delusion" and buyers of high-discount funds "will have the numbers against them" (S-338). The cost structure that produces that answer is published in the mechanism itself — the discount is the compensation for the liquidity benefit net of the manager's fees (S-337) — so a candidate would have to re-derive a cost floor that the literature has already priced. The residual, if any, is a **cross-sectional** selection inside a published distribution: which fund trades at the widest discount, and the published cross-section answers that the widest discounts are the ones that subsequently underperform (S-338, S-340).

**Reason 5 — the decision-time state is stale, and the effective sample is small and regime-dependent.** The state a candidate needs — the **current** discount — is not knowable when it would have to be used: "the NAVs of the BDCs underlying the index are **unknown on such dates and will only be published in the financial reporting of the BDC several weeks after the NAV date**" (S-340), and the same article documents a **foresight-bias** convention around the index construction. In real time the observable is the **market price**, not the discount; the discount is a *published, lagged* quantity. The effective sample is small and the regime is episodic: 47 listed BDCs over December 2009 – June 2022 (S-340), 11 Swedish closed-end funds over 2000–2012 (S-338), a roughly three-year index-exclusion window (S-339), the 2020 stress episode and the first half of 2022 (S-340). A candidate would be claiming a magnitude inside a regime whose trigger is a dated, published event, and the discount's *level* is only observable with a reporting lag.

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (a) a **published, decision-time** state is shown to determine the **sign or the existence** (not merely the magnitude) of a price-to-NAV discount in a vehicle that lacks a creation/redemption mechanism, and that state is not itself a lagged publication; (b) the published negative result on the discount trade (S-338, S-340) is **answered rather than assumed away**, with the answer not resting on a *published* historical sample of the same kind; (c) a **published** account of a discount-closing mechanism is shown **not** to exist in the sub-family or vehicle class being claimed, given the statutory repurchase route (S-342) and the below-NAV issuance constraint (S-339, S-340, S-342); and (d) the **current listing status, disclosure cadence and net-asset-value publication rules** of the specific vehicle class are verified from documents that can be fetched and text-extracted — the register notes that the BDC NAV publication lag is currently evidenced only by an **author's methodological statement** (S-340) and not by a **regulatory or rulebook source**, which is a **named, dated verification target** rather than an assumption.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-050).** The closed-end-fund / BDC price-to-NAV discount family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 87**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; **seven `S-` identifiers were consumed (S-337 … S-343)**; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of data availability — the family failed on the *already-published-headline* limb (reason 1), the *sign-and-existence* limb (reason 2), the *structurally-blocked-arbitrage* limb (reason 3), the *cost-floor* limb (reason 4) and the *decision-time-observability-and-sample* limb (reason 5). The screen's product is a **rediscovery guard** (row 87) and a fourth registered precedent that the *mechanism-first* operation can be run on a genuinely new family; it is also the register's first screen of a family in which the **discount is permanent by design** rather than a mechanical widening, which is the structural mirror of the parked IDEA-010 residual.


## R-051 — Catastrophe bonds and insurance-linked securities (ILS): a modelled risk premium with a delayed, illiquid market (Cycle 90 family screen — bounded negative; **re-issued in Cycle 91**, and supplied with line-verified grounds)

**Identifier note (state-integrity repair, Cycle 91).** This screen was executed in Cycle 90 and its record
was written into this file under the identifier **R-041** — an identifier **Cycle 61 had already
consumed** for the published-count/queue-state template (D) screen (baseline row 70), so the file briefly
held **51 headings but only 50 distinct identifiers**. Following the register's standing discipline — a
consumed identifier is written for real under its original number and a **fresh** one is minted, as with
**R-017** in Cycle 37 and **R-025** in Cycle 51 — the Cycle-61 entry **stands at R-041** and this screen is
re-issued as **R-051**. The **verdict is unchanged**; what Cycle 91 changed is the **evidence under it**:
the Cycle-90 record rested on **no source at all** — it described the premium as "well-documented" without
naming a document — and so it did not meet the register's own standard that a bounded negative must rest on
line-verified material. The five reasons below are therefore stated afresh against **three
line-verified sources** (S-353, S-354, S-355). **No verdict, baseline row count or score changed by the
re-issue.**

**What was screened.** **Catastrophe bonds (CAT bonds) and related insurance-linked securities** —
privately placed bonds whose coupons pay a spread above a modelled expected loss, with trigger-based
structures (indemnity, industry loss, parametric, modelled loss) and settlement that is often delayed
until the triggering event is verified.

**Why the family was worth screening now.** The family is **absent from the known-baseline table** and from
every earlier screen (the state-first templates A–I, R-039 … R-046, and the mechanism-first family screens
R-047 … R-050), and it
was named in the Cycle-89 handoff as a family to screen. Its shape is the register's recurring
**premium-and-structure** shape: a compensation paid by one side of a bilateral market for bearing a
risk that the other side can model.

**Sources.** **S-353** (Braun & Kousky, "Catastrophe Bonds", Wharton Impact, July 2021 — **line-verified**;
an educational primer, **not peer-reviewed**, and used only for the instrument's published mechanics); **S-354**
(Ando, Fu, Roch & Wiriadinata, "Sovereign Climate Debt Instruments: An Overview of the Green and Catastrophe
Bond Markets", IMF Staff Climate Note 2022/004 — **line-verified**; an IMF staff note, **not peer-reviewed**,
and used for the published premium accounting and the market's published barriers); **S-355** (Supreme Court
of Bermuda, Commercial Court, 2020: No. 369, *Aeolus Re Ltd v CS ILS SICAV-SIF et al*, [2022] SC (Bda) 30 Com
— **line-verified**; a **primary legal document** on an industry-loss trigger's resolution architecture). The
Cycle-90 record's own unsupported assertions are **not** carried forward: no document is cited here that was
not fetched and text-extracted in Cycle 91.

**Reason 1 — the premium is a published, quantified baseline, so the headline is claimed.** The spread above
expected loss is documented **with a magnitude**: "Empirically, the cedents pay more than they receive in
expectation. According to Artemis … **the investors' average return to catastrophe bond in coupon is around
2 to 4 times the expected loss**", and the published comparison is explicitly benchmark-relative — "**Whether
the positive spread is expensive depends on the benchmark to compare**" (S-354). A candidate in this family
would be re-presenting a documented premium at a published multiple of expected loss — a baseline, not a
discovery.

**Reason 2 — the state that moves the spread is a published, *retrospective* event, not a decision-time
variable.** The published account of the spread's own variation is event-driven and backward-looking:
"**risk spreads have widened materially following major catastrophes in the past**, such as 2006 following a
US hurricane and 2012 following earthquakes in Japan and New Zealand" (S-354). The spread-widening
episodes are dated events whose effect is measured *after* the fact; nothing in the published record offers a
state that is known *before* the decision and that flips the sign or the existence of an unclaimed effect
(S-353's account of the four trigger types adds no such state). The family therefore fails the register's
standing **G-13** test (is the state published *before* the decision?) on the published record's own
terms.

**Reason 3 — the sign-determining quantity is a third-party *estimate*, resolved over a long published
development period, so the condition is not determinable ex ante.** The instrument pays when the
*estimated* loss crosses a trigger, and the resolution architecture is documented in a primary legal
record: the loss "size was either **not yet known** or was reported by the agreed report publisher at an
amount which was not large enough to trigger the payment obligation", in which case "**a Development Period
applied**, namely a further time period in which to monitor whether the estimated insured loss developed
sufficiently in order to trigger a payment … By clause 9(f) of the Swap Confirmations, **the Development
Period End Date was 18 months after the Risk Period End Date**" (S-355). The market's most-used trigger type
is also the slowest: "**Indemnity triggers typically involve a relatively lengthy payout process, since loss
reporting and verification must be accomplished before the release of funds. Nonetheless, the indemnity
trigger is by far the most widely used trigger type in today's catastrophe bond market**" (S-353). The
sign-determining condition is thus not observable at decision time at all — it is settled months to years
later by third parties.

**Reason 4 — the premium's benchmark is a model output, and the data that would improve it are published as
scarce.** "Issuance of a catastrophe bond typically requires engaging a **specialized modeling firm** to
quantify the catastrophe risk. The risk modeler will estimate the probability that the conditions for the
payout would be met over the term of the bond … The modeling firm also provides an estimate of the expected
loss for investors … **Such a model-based approach to risk assessment is needed, since historical data on the
most extreme natural disasters are typically too scarce for a reliable estimation of the outer tail of the
loss distribution**" (S-353); the IMF note names the same thing as a market barrier — "**the requirement of
complicated underlying catastrophe models**" (S-354). The quantity that sets the premium is therefore a
**private, revisable model estimate**, not a published decision-time series, and the published record
states that the underlying data do not exist in sufficient depth.

**Reason 5 — the cost floor and the market's shallowness bind, and the information asymmetry is published.**
"While green and catastrophe bonds have gained significant popularity, **their markets remain fairly
shallow** at the sovereign level", and the note records "**significant barriers to the scale up of the
catastrophe bond market: high transaction costs**, the requirement of complicated underlying catastrophe
models, and **a narrow investor base**" (S-354); the investor base itself is narrow and specialised (S-353).
The published information asymmetry compounds the cost floor: "For investors, indemnity triggers can be
**relatively opaque**, since they do not have direct access to information on the sponsor's insurance
portfolio. This raises a moral hazard concern" (S-353). A premium of the published size, in a market of
this depth, with this opacity, does not clear a cost-and-information floor.

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (a) a **published, decision-time** state is shown to
determine the **sign or the existence** (not merely the magnitude) of a CAT-bond return after costs, and that
state is neither a lagged publication nor a private model output; (b) the **published premium account** is
answered rather than assumed away (the "2 to 4 times the expected loss" figure and the benchmark-relative
question, S-354); (c) a **settlement architecture** with a materially shorter, published and observable
determination of the trigger is documented, so that the sign-determining condition becomes knowable before
the decision (against the 18-month development period of S-355 and the indemnity process of S-353); and (d) a
**liquidity and cost record** is obtained from a document that can be fetched and text-extracted — the
Cycle-91 route attempts for the venue-level and market-volume documents failed and are recorded as honest
negatives, so the market's depth and cost remain **documented but not measured** here.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-051).** The catastrophe-bond / ILS family is recorded in the
known-baseline table (**row 88**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; **three `S-`
identifiers were consumed (S-353 … S-355)** in this re-issue, none in Cycle 90; no score changed; no
validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of
data availability — the family failed on the *already-published-premium* limb (reason 1), the
*retrospective-state* limb (reason 2), the *unresolvable-sign-condition* limb (reason 3), the
*model-dependence* limb (reason 4) and the *cost-and-shallow-market* limb (reason 5). The screen's product
is a **rediscovery guard** (row 88) and a registered precedent that the **premium-and-structure** family
shape can be screened to the same standard as the others.

## R-052 — Freight-derivative contract pricing: the dry-bulk FFA forward curve and the container-freight futures family (Cycle 91 family screen — bounded negative)

**What was screened.** The register's **sixth mechanism-first family screen** (after R-047 in Cycle 75,
R-048 in Cycle 80, R-049 in Cycle 87, R-050 in Cycle 88 and R-051 in Cycle 90): the
**freight-derivative contract-pricing family** — the published forward curve of the Baltic Exchange's **dry-bulk Forward Freight Agreements**
(Capesize, Panamax, Supramax and Handysize time-charter and voyage routes), the **container-freight
futures listed on the Shanghai International Energy Exchange** (the SCFIS (Europe service) contract), and
the **published container spot indices** (the SSE's SCFI/SCFIS family) against which those contracts settle.
The family's shape is a **risk premium embedded in a forward price**: a charterer or shipper who is long
physical freight exposure pays a forward rate above the expected future spot to the counterparty who takes
the risk, and the forward curve is also the market's information-bearing price for future physical
conditions.

**Why the family was worth screening now.** The family is **absent from the known-baseline table** and from
every earlier screen (the state-first templates A–I, R-039 … R-046, and the mechanism-first family screens
R-047 … R-051): no row
covers freight, shipping, FFA, Baltic assessments or container-freight futures, and the whole file
contains no prior mention of the family outside the register's own new material. It was named in the
